clusters on flight control, control allocation and handling qualities, and on lift fans, ducted fans and the fan in the wing.

### Powered lift, V/STOL and STOVL aircraft

Powered-lift aircraft of every kind, from the period jet-lift and lift-fan concepts and the Harrier to tiltrotors, tailsitters and the hover and transition of unmanned vertical-takeoff vehicles. **Of these records, 544 were published in 2001 or earlier, and 233 in 2015 or later.**

### Flight control, control allocation and handling qualities

Flight control of powered-lift, hovering and over-actuated vehicles, control allocation among redundant effectors, integrated flight and propulsion control, and the handling qualities of hover and transition. **Of these records, 112 were published in 2001 or earlier, and 152 in 2015 or later.**

### Jet impingement, ground effect and hot gas ingestion

Jet impingement on surfaces, ground effect, fountains, outwash, hot gas ingestion and the jet footprint, with the flow and heat transfer of a free gas jet on a plate, which is the physics of deck heating. **Of these records, 205 were published in 2001 or earlier, and 88 in 2015 or later.**

**15 records.** \[[Beermann et al 2015][research_beermann_hofler_2015]\] \[[Cangioli et al 2018][research_cangioli_pennacchi_2018]\] \[[Dellacorte and Steinetz 1992][research_dellacorte_steinetz_1992]\] \[[El 1983][research_d_1983]\] \[[Fellenstein et al 1996][research_fellenstein_dellacorte_1996]\] \[[Gardner 1999][research_gardner_1999]\] \[[Goff et al 2015][research_goff_sheridan_2015]\] \[[Kerr and Bensmiller 2024][research_kerr_bensmiller_2024]\] \[[More and Datta 2003][research_more_datta_2003]\] \[[Oswald et al 2004][research_oswald_mullen_2004]\] \[[Proctor et al 2002][research_proctor_kumar_2002]\] \[[Salehi and Heshmat 2000][research_salehi_heshmat_2000]\] \[[Su et al 2023][research_su_huang_2023]\] \[[Taylor et al 2004][research_taylor_dunlap_2004]\] \[[Wittig et al 1994][research_wittig_waschka_1994]\]

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

### The Deflationary Reading Can Be Pushed Too Far

**The argument above is capable of proving too much.** If one insists that a demonstration teaching the
engineers nothing new is theatre, then almost every public demonstration in aviation history is theatre,
which is a sign the criterion is wrong rather than a discovery about aviation.

**A demonstration has a second, legitimate function, which is to establish that separately verified capabilities compose.**
Engineers frequently find that they do not. Doing the three in sequence tests the aircraft's ability to do
them in sequence, including the fuel state, the thermal state, the pilot's workload and the systems'
behaviour after a long, busy sortie.
**None of those is captured by the arithmetic in this article, and all of them are real.**

### Mach 1.05 Is the Worst Place on the Drag Curve, Not the Easiest

**The claim that the supersonic leg was cheap deserves its strongest counter-argument stated properly.**
Transonic drag rises steeply and peaks a little above Mach one, so Mach 1.05 is close to the most expensive
point on the whole drag curve rather than a comfortable cruise. An aircraft that reaches Mach 1.05 in level
flight has demonstrated that it can push through the peak, which is a stiffer test than reaching a higher
Mach number would be once past it.

**That counter-argument is correct on drag and does not disturb the thermal conclusion**, which is separate
and which the arithmetic settles. It also does not disturb the ordering conclusion.
**A conclusion that survives its own best counter-argument is worth more than one that needed the argument suppressed.**

### The Assumed Inputs and What They Carry

Several results here rest on quantities nobody published, and which conclusions would move if the
assumptions moved should be set out explicitly.

| Assumed quantity | Used for | Does the conclusion depend on it? |
|---|---|---|
| Fan polar moment of inertia, 25 to 50 kg·m² | Clutch energy magnitude | No. The halving identity is exact and the time constant is under a fifth of a second across the bracket |
| Stage loading coefficient of 0.40 | Estimating fan speed | Weakly. Every threshold in the counter-rotation table is an ordinary fan speed regardless |
| Roll-post arm, 2.5 to 4.0 m | Counter-rotation threshold | No. The conclusion is stated across the whole bracket |
| Zero-lift drag 0.025, rolling friction 0.03, nozzle aft until unstick | Ground roll | Slightly. The minimum of 307 to 380 feet stays well short of 500 across the weight bracket |
| Rear nozzle at its published 18,000 lbf at eighty knots | The Mission X unstick coefficient | No. The finding is that the record cannot pin a coefficient, and a smaller nozzle thrust only raises the bound of 0.206 |
| Transonic drag coefficient 0.035 | Dash thrust margin | No. The margin is 2.389 and would survive a doubling |
| Sortie fuel burn of 3,500 lb | The floor of the takeoff-weight bracket and the landing weight margin | The gain figure, yes. The finding that the ordering helps does not, since the gain exceeds unity for any positive burn |
| Fan to nozzle separation of 7.5 m | Moment arms and the centre-of-gravity band | Only for the arms in metres. **The station as a fraction, 0.4737, needs no separation at all** |
| Thrust split authority of 10 percent | The pitching-moment threshold | Weakly. It sets the threshold height, and the finding is that the threshold is near a metre rather than far from it |
| Radius of gyration at 0.20 to 0.25 of span | Roll control power | No. The bracket is reported and the residual after a single-rotation reaction is derisory across all of it |
| Oswald efficiency of 0.75, zero-lift drag 0.018, wave drag 0.017 | The dash drag build-up | No. Induced drag is 0.0027 of a total 0.0377, so the build-up is insensitive to it |
| Thrust lapse exponent of 0.7 | Thrust available at altitude | No. The sensitivity is tabulated and the margin survives 0.6 to 1.0 |
| Clutch plate mass of 20 kg | The plate temperature rise | No. It scales the rise linearly and the conclusion is that the bound is absurd, not that the rise is a particular number |
| Clutch engagement time of 9 s | The power bound on clutch dissipation | No. The bound exceeds the dissipation for any engagement longer than about a third of a second |

### A Demonstration Is Not Only an Experiment

**The framing this whole series uses treats a research aircraft as an instrument for answering a question, and the X-35 does not fit that framing cleanly.**
It was a competitor as much as an instrument. Its purpose included persuading a customer, and a machine
built partly to persuade is not well described by asking only what it measured.
**The framing does not break down so much as become incomplete**, and the incompleteness is exactly the
space in which Mission X sits.

## The Source Base

### Both Halves, Counted and Not Only Divided

**The count and the fraction are reported together, because they move independently and reporting only the fraction misrepresents both.**
Adding contemporary sources leaves the period count untouched while lowering its share, and adding period
sources does the same to the contemporary share. Neither movement is a fact about coverage.

**The research survey holds 4,297 research records drawn from Crossref, the NASA Technical Reports Server and the Defense Technical Information Center.**
Of these, 2,043, or 47.5 percent, were published through 2001, the year the competition was decided,
and 1,990, or 46.3 percent, were published from 2015 onward. Of the contemporary half, 960 records
were published from 2022 onward. **The median publication year of the 4,287 dated records is 2003.**

### The Period Vocabulary for This Article's Derived Subjects

**Several subjects this article's equations name are filed in the period literature under other words.**
The mechanics beneath an equation are not the same literature as the technology above it. This article says
momentum drag, and the period reports say **inlet momentum drag, ram drag, lift engine installation losses
and propulsion-induced effects**. This article says canted joint, and the period says
**swivel duct, deflector, skewed axis coupling and spatial mechanism**. The records cited under those
subjects are filed under the period's words, and the period writes installation effects in the plural, so a
reader looking for them should search in the period's vocabulary rather than the article's.

### A Designation With Almost No Literature of Its Own

**This is the sharpest finding the source base contains.**

**Two research records this article cites carry "X-35" in their titles, and both are papers of 2002.**
They treat the X-35B's flight control law design and flying qualities in
[Walker and Allen 2002][research_walker_allen_2002] and the jet effects test effort in
[Buchholz 2002][research_buchholz_2002]. A paper of the same year, [Roberts 2002][research_roberts_2002], names
the rival's X-32B. No later research title carries either designation.

**The previous two articles found something that looked similar and means the opposite.** The X-33's cluster
held sixty records, of which the X-33 article reports fifty-seven predate 2002. The X-34's held its records and every one predated 2002.
Both programmes were cancelled, and the conclusion drawn was that a cancelled programme stops generating
literature under its own name, so the documentary trace of a vehicle measures how long it survived rather
than what it contributed.

**The X-35 is the control case for that claim and it fails it in an unexpected direction.** This programme
was not cancelled. It won, and its architecture went into production and is flying now.
**And its designation has a smaller documentary trace than either of the cancelled vehicles, because it barely had one.**

What the literature carries instead is the programme name and the product name. Counted by title among the
research records this article cites, 56 name the Joint Strike Fighter in full or as JSF, the dated ones
running from 1996 to 2026. Twenty name the F-35, the dated ones running from 2003 to
2019, and fourteen of those also name the Joint Strike Fighter.
**Beyond those two papers of 2002, nothing names the aeroplane.**

**The inference this supports is narrower than it first appears, and it is worth stating carefully.** The
X-35 designation was applied to competition demonstrators built by a contractor and flown for a source
selection, and that is not the institutional arrangement that produces published reports. The X-33 and the
X-34 were agency programmes with agency reporting obligations, which is why their cancelled programmes still
left a paper trail.
**So the finding is about who was writing rather than about who won, and the three cases together say that the documentary trace measures the institution rather than the aircraft.**

### The Reference Works

The following books and monographs were drawn on for the standing theory rather than for the aircraft,
covering propulsion, powered lift, rotor and fan aerodynamics, aircraft performance and flight test method.
**Almost none of them is about the X-35 and that is the point**, since the relations this article displays
are older than the aeroplane and better documented than it.

### What the Record Does Not Contain

There is no published flight test report for the X-35B, and the standard survey of the
[American X-vehicles][ref_xvehicles] treats the type in a page. The two papers of 2002 named above are
the only research records whose titles name the aircraft. There is no published weight statement, no
published engine deck, no published fan map, and no published moment of inertia for the lift fan.
**The quantitative reconstruction in this article is built entirely from performance figures released for publicity and from the physics that connects them**,
and the article says so at every point where an assumption was needed.

### The Definition of Primary

Primary for this subject means the reports and papers written during the competition and the technology work
that preceded it, together with the contemporary programme announcements that record what was flown and
when. **The flight chronology in this article rests on the latter**, because the demonstrator campaign was a
contractor activity and its detailed test reports are not public.

**For counting, primacy is read from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the
NASA Technical Reports Server or it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which
Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. By that rule 646 of the
4,297 research records, or 15.0 percent, are report primaries. **The rule does not reach the
conference papers of the competition period**, such as the inventor's own papers and the two papers of
2002 on the X-35B, so 15.0 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is first-hand.

### Thin Subjects

**Seven subjects are thin in the cited literature, and they are not all thin for the same reason.**
A subject can be thin because the work was never done, because the heading is wrong, or because the
knowledge is so settled that it stopped generating papers, a distinction the X-34 article draws.

The counts are taken over the research records this article cites, so that anyone can repeat them. A record
counts for a subject when its title contains one of the phrases in the second column, matched without regard
to case and with a hyphen read as a space, and a record is modern when it was published in 2015 or later. A
phrase count also catches a title that uses the phrase in another sense, such as an installed thrust reverser under the thrust lapse phrases,
and it misses work filed under another heading, which is what the last column records.

| Subject | Title phrases counted | Records | Which kind of thin |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ground roll and takeoff distance | ground roll, takeoff distance, take-off distance, rolling friction | 1, none modern | **Settled.** A closed-form result in every performance textbook, and this article derives it in two lines |
| Disc loading | disc loading, disk loading | 10, one modern | **Settled.** Nine of the ten date from 1948 to 1971, when disc loading was the parameter by which VTOL concepts were compared, and the subject now sits inside momentum theory and lift fan work |
| Jet footprint and ground erosion | ground erosion, surface erosion, deck heating, jet footprint, impingement pressure | 3, one modern | **Wrong heading.** It lives inside impingement and ground environment work, in the cluster on jet impingement, ground effect and hot gas ingestion |
| Rotor spin-up and inertia | spin-up, spin up, spinup, rotational inertia, polar moment, flywheel | 0, none modern | **Wrong heading.** It lives inside drive system transient work |
| Thrust lapse with altitude | thrust lapse, installed thrust, altitude performance | 8, one modern | **Wrong heading.** It lives inside installed performance and engine decks |
| Stagnation temperature and kinetic heating | stagnation temperature, recovery temperature, kinetic heating, aerodynamic heating | 8, two modern | **Moved.** The six older records date from 1949 to 1968 and treat the kinetic heating of aircraft structures, and by title the two modern ones are heating prediction tools for high-speed and supersonic vehicle design rather than studies of the transonic case |
| Standard atmosphere | standard atmosphere, atmospheric model, hydrostatic | 2, one modern | **Settled.** Only one of the two records was published in 2015 or later |

**One homonym is worth naming because it falls on the article's own term of art.**
Figure of merit is a standard quantity in thermoelectrics, where it is written as a dimensionless number,
and in plasmonic and photonic sensing.
**In the contemporary literature, hover efficiency and figure of merit also name properties of solar cells and graphene sensors**,
and the thermoelectric and photonic records are excluded from this survey.

**None of these is padded and none is reported as a gap in the field.** Four of the seven are headings
rather than subjects, and the article cites the literature where it actually lives.
**The three that are genuinely settled rather than misheaded are the ground roll, disc loading and the standard atmosphere**,
and across the three the article cites two records under their phrases published in 2015 or later.

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject.** Much of
this subject's vocabulary also names something else, and a record that shares only such a word is excluded.
Acquisition also names corporate mergers, biomedical signal recording and seismic surveying. Figure of
merit also names a quantity in thermoelectrics, photonics and power electronics. Ski jump also names dam
spillways and a sport, downwash also names chimney and building plumes, and outwash also names glacial
outwash plains. Engine performance at altitude also names diesel and gasoline engines for road vehicles,
and erosion also names the erosion and corrosion of metals by liquid and slurry jets. Hot gas ingestion also
names the ingress of hot gas through a turbine rim seal, which is a different flow from the reingestion of a
lift jet's exhaust. Harrier also names a bird, swivel also names furniture, textiles and a dictionary headword,
and electric propulsion also names the ion, Hall and plasma thrusters of spacecraft. Impinging jets of water,
liquid fuel, sprays and quenching flows share a word with the jet footprint and none of its subject.
Correction, erratum, retraction and withdrawal notices, figure, table and supplementary-material records,
peer-review reports, conference programmes, news items and journal front matter are excluded as well, because
a survey counts research works and those are parts of works or editorial events rather than works. Reviews of
books, unsigned reference entries, reference entries of two pages or fewer, and the abstracts of papers, patents
and reports reprinted in the abstract sections of other journals are excluded on the same ground, while a signed
encyclopedia article and a textbook chapter are research works and stay.

**Some records of doubtful relevance are kept.** Underwater vehicle and marine vessel thrust allocation,
planetary gearboxes, counter-rotating pumps and turbines, the kinematics of spatial mechanisms and
manipulators, gas jets impinging on plates and automotive clutches each share the control-allocation,
kinematic, ground-environment or drivetrain problem this article treats. The clutch records are kept in
particular because the drivetrain literature that owns the clutch is largely automotive, but only where they treat
the engagement, its energy or its heat. Electrical machines,
batteries and power electronics written for electric aircraft are excluded, because their subject is the
machine rather than the aircraft, while aircraft-level studies of battery and powertrain sizing are kept. A
reader may weigh the doubtful classes accordingly.

**A record whose only link to this subject is general aeronautics is excluded, and so is one about a vehicle class the argument never uses.**
Hypersonic and combined-cycle propulsion, rockets, launch vehicles and spacecraft, rotor dynamics and
helicopter airworthiness, aircraft systems, maintenance, health monitoring and instrumentation, airline and
airport operations, the noise of transport engines and airframes, transport, supersonic-transport and
high-lift aerodynamics, boundary-layer physics, combustion and emissions, hydrogen storage, railway and
road-vehicle aerodynamics, road-vehicle torque allocation, automotive and marine transmissions, torque converters
and shift control, gear manufacturing and gearbox noise, engine accessories, jet impingement in cooling systems and
heat exchangers, flapping-wing and lighter-than-air craft, and design studies of unrelated configurations therefore
fall outside the survey, as do bibliographies, proceedings volumes, and comments and discussions on papers. A record on a method or a phenomenon the argument relies on
is kept whatever vehicle it concerns, which is why rocket plumes on soil, ejectors of any working fluid and
boundary layer ingestion on transport aircraft remain.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.**
A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles
only.

## Epistemic State

### Historical Fact

The X-35A first flew on 24 October 2000 and completed its conventional campaign on 22 November 2000. The
X-35C first flew on 16 December 2000 and completed 73 flights and 58 hours by 11 March 2001. The X-35A was
rebuilt as the X-35B, which made its first vertical takeoff and landing on 23 June 2001 and completed
testing on 30 July 2001. On 9 July 2001 it performed its first in-flight conversion to hovering mode,
refuelled in the air, and reached Mach 1.08 on the same sortie. On 16 July 2001 it flew a short takeoff, a
transition and a vertical landing. On 20 July 2001 Major Art Tomassetti flew Mission X, and on 26 July 2001
Simon Hargreaves repeated it, and on a later flight that day landed vertically at a weight over 34,000
pounds. The contract was awarded to Lockheed Martin on
26 October 2001. The lift system's published figures are a fifty-inch two-stage counter-rotating fan
producing twenty thousand pounds force from twenty-nine thousand shaft horsepower, a three-bearing swivel
module passing eighteen thousand pounds force and rotating ninety-five degrees in 2.5 seconds, and roll
posts of 1,950 pounds force each.

### Engineering Analysis

The fan's figure of merit of 0.697, its induced velocity of 169.3 metres per second, its mass flow of 579.2
pounds per second and its disc loading of 70.2 kilopascals follow from published geometry and performance by
momentum theory. The augmentation ratio of 1.676 and the implied core mass flow of 320.2 pounds per second
follow from the square-root law. The identity that a hovering jet's dynamic pressure equals its disc loading
is exact. The identity that exactly half the energy drawn through a clutch engaging a stationary inertia is
destroyed is exact and independent of the torque profile. The stagnation temperature of 291.2 kelvin at Mach
1.05 and twenty-five thousand feet follows from the standard atmosphere and the energy equation. The unstick
force balance shows that the record cannot pin a wing lift coefficient, since the lift system
vectored to 60 degrees carries all but at most a coefficient of 0.206 at the heaviest weight in the bracket.
The 26 July nozzle deflection is therefore given as a table over weight and coefficient rather than as a
single angle. The minimum ground roll of 307.4 to 380.0 feet follows from the momentum balance with the
nozzle aft, and the 200 foot Mission X roll in the pilot's account is shown to be incompatible with an
eighty-knot unstick at any weight in the bracket. The coefficient of 0.537 to 0.841 at the 180 knot
conversion assumes sea-level density, as the momentum drag does.

The momentum drag of 5,469 pounds force at 180 knots is the fan's mass flow times the true airspeed and is
exact given that flow. The inlet temperature rise of 66.95 kelvin that consumes the hover margin is an
inversion of a corrected-flow relation. The centre-of-gravity station at 47.37 percent of the fan-to-nozzle
distance follows from the hover moment balance and needs no separation in metres. The canted-joint relation
is exact geometry, and the finding that series joints add only when their cants alternate was established by
searching the roll space rather than by derivation. The roll control power of 46.07 degrees per second
squared and the residual 3.083 rest on an estimated moment of inertia and are reported across a bracket.

**Every figure in this section except the series-joint search result can be recomputed from the relations and inputs the article displays**,
and the inputs that were assumed rather than published are listed in the assumptions table with what each
one carries.

### Inference

That Mission X was the assembly of already-demonstrated elements rather than the retirement of technical
risk is an inference from the flight chronology, and it is a strong one, because the programme's own
contemporary statements list every element as already flown four days earlier. That the genuinely novel
event was the in-flight conversion of 9 July is an inference of the same kind. That counter-rotation was
necessary rather than merely desirable is an inference from an inverted threshold calculation across a
bracket of assumed roll-post positions. That the short takeoff was a technique demonstration rather than a
necessity follows from the thrust margin of 1.232 at 34,000 pounds and is close to a deduction at that
weight, but no Mission X takeoff weight is published, and at the ceiling of the bracket, empty weight plus
full internal fuel, the margin is 1.0096 and the deduction fails.
**That the conversion was the expensive event is an inference from the momentum drag**, which is a real
force with a real magnitude, but the step from a large force to a hard piloting task is an inference and not
a measurement, since no workload data for these sorties is public.

### What the Record Does Not Settle

**Whether the fly-off would have gone the other way without Mission X is not settled and cannot be.** Source
selection weighs many things, the flight was three months before the decision, and no public document
apportions the decision among its inputs.

**Whether the X-32 could have flown an equivalent profile with more development time is not settled.** Its
architecture required reconfiguration as built, but the question of whether that was fundamental or
contingent is a design question that was never taken far enough to answer.

**Several magnitudes are not settled because the quantities they need have never been published.** The
clutch energies need the fan's moment of inertia, the moment arms in metres need the fan-to-nozzle
separation, the roll control power needs the roll moment of inertia, and the pitching moment of the momentum
drag needs the height of the fan inlet above the centre of gravity.
**In every case the article gives the relation that needs none of them beside the number that does**, and
the assumptions table in the framing section says which conclusions would move.

**Whether the demonstration's persuasive function should count as part of its engineering value is a question of judgement rather than fact**,
and this article deliberately answers it in two directions in two different sections.

## Out of Scope

The production F-35 programme, its cost and schedule history, and the 2004 weight reduction effort are
outside this article, which is about a demonstrator that flew in 2001. The low-observable design of either
competitor is outside it. The avionics and sensor fusion that dominate the production aircraft's reputation
are outside it, because the demonstrators carried none. The carrier variant's approach and arrestment
characteristics are touched on only where they bear on the variant weight argument. The source selection
process itself, and the construct-validity argument about demonstrators generally, are covered in the
article on the X-32 and are not repeated here.

## Conclusion

**The X-35 answered the question of whether a supersonic fighter can hover without being rebuilt between the two, and the answer is yes, by carrying a fan it does not use most of the time.**

The fan is worth carrying because thrust rises as the square root of mass flow at fixed power, so moving air
the engine never touched multiplies vertical thrust by 1.676 without burning more fuel. It is
counter-rotating because a single-rotation fan of that power would have consumed most or all of the
aircraft's roll authority merely to stand still. Its exhaust is cold because the landing surface and the
inlet both care more about temperature and pressure than about thrust, and the fan presses between half and
three fifths as hard as the core does at a third of the absolute temperature.
**At 34,000 pounds that barrier is worth 66.95 kelvin of tolerable inlet temperature rise before the aircraft can no longer hold itself up.**

**Mission X, the flight the aircraft is remembered for, was not where any of that was established.** Every
element of it had been flown before 20 July 2001, the first in-flight conversion had happened eleven days
earlier on a sortie that went faster, and the arithmetic shows that none of the three events was
individually demanding for an aircraft with 41,900 pounds force of vertical thrust at the 34,000 pounds it is
documented to have weighed on 26 July. No Mission X weight is published, and the short takeoff was flown by
an aeroplane that could have gone straight up unless it carried nearly full internal fuel. The supersonic dash raised the
stagnation temperature 3.1 kelvin above a standard sea-level day. The vertical landing happened at the
lightest weight of the sortie, in the ordering that makes it easiest at any takeoff weight in the bracket.

**And the expensive event is not among the three.** Engaging the fan at the 180 knots the aircraft converted
at costs 5,469 pounds force of momentum drag, which is 30.4 percent of everything the rear nozzle can
produce, applied above and ahead of the centre of gravity where it pitches as well as decelerates.
**Nothing in the hat trick produces a step change of that size.**

**The sortie was a demonstration in the exact sense of the word, and that is not a criticism.** It made an
architectural property legible in one flight to people who would never read a test report, and it did so
honestly, because the property was real and the rival did not have it.
**What it was not is the experiment.** The experiment was the clutch engaging in flight on 9 July, and
nobody remembers the date.
